# T20 — ADMIN SERVER ROUTES (PART B) + THE OWNER ROUTES · phases P09501–P10000

**Territory (write access):** the last 24 `app/api/admin/**/route.ts` files
(`find app/api/admin -name route.ts | sort | tail -24`) · all 12 `app/api/owner/**/route.ts` files.
36 files, 8,076 lines. Everything else in the repo was read only.

**Branch:** `sweep6/t20-admin-api-b` · **worktree:** `~/Documents/Projects/sweep6/T20` · **port:** 4120

## The budget these 500 phases were written to

| block | ids | share | what it is |
|-------|-----|-------|------------|
| A | P09501–P09700 | 40% | reading the code for correctness |
| B | P09701–P09800 | 20% | conformance to the project rules named in the prompt |
| C | P09801–P09875 | 15% | watching it run (headless Playwright, port 4120) |
| D | P09876–P09925 | 10% | screenshots actually opened and read (1280×800 + A35 360×780 dpr3) |
| E | P09926–P09975 | 10% | tracing a change across panels |
| F | P09976–P10000 | 5% | judgment — does a real restaurant need it to work this way |

**Per-file floor.** All 36 files are named in the scope, so each gets **ten or more**: five
correctness reads (block A), a sign-in gate row and an egress row (block B), and at least three
more from the live / screenshot / trace / judgment blocks. The two biggest —
`app/api/owner/reports/route.ts` (1,118 lines) and `app/api/owner/staff/route.ts` (1,142) — carry
the most, and `app/api/admin/restaurants/access-tree/route.ts` and
`app/api/admin/restaurants/settings/route.ts` each carry their own named findings.

**Result key:** ✅ pass · ❌ fail · ⏭ skipped, with a written reason.

**The result column is the SECOND full pass**, re-run from the top after every fix landed. Six rows
read ❌ on the first pass; each says so in its note, names exactly what was wrong, and is green here
because the fix is in this PR.

**Restoration rule.** Every write phase below runs against **French House** and restores what it
changed in a `finally` block. **Aangan is read-only** and was never written to; block E's last trace
re-reads its whole settings row before and after a French House write and diffs it. Rows this run
created were deleted by id in the same run.

**The restore proves itself.** On the FIRST pass two restores were wrong and left French House
changed: the delivery-channel restore sent `key: null` when that route only clears on an empty
STRING, so a throwaway key survived; and the banquet counter's "before" was captured AFTER the write,
so it restored the new value over the old. Both were found by re-reading the row rather than trusting
the restore, both are fixed in the harness, and the harness now FAILS its own run if French House is
not back. Final state verified field by field: 13/13 as found. The one deliberate exception is table
1's QR code — rotating it is what that button is for, and the old code is meant to stop working.

**Three of my own assertions were wrong before any code was.** Each is recorded on its row rather
than quietly corrected: the bill/parcel preview documents have no literal `<html>` tag (valid HTML5,
the element is implied); French House genuinely HAS table-types handed to its owner, so a 200 there is
correct; and `/owner/logs` does not exist — the route is `/owner/activity`. A test that asserts the
wrong thing is worse than no test.

**One gate is RED for a reason that is not mine.** `npm run verify:read-guards` fails on a clean
`origin/main`: `cancel_classified` and `cancel_classify_failed` (migration 337) are in
`components/admin/shared.tsx`'s `ACT_LABEL` and missing from `lib/logTrail.ts`, so those two rows read
"System › Other". Both files are outside my territory — see the 🔗 HANDOFF in
`.claude/sweep/T20-findings.md`. My own three checks in that file pass.

The only delete is a soft delete that also writes the Removals audit, and the export keeps the money trail. Checked against docs/COMPLIANCE-GUARDRAILS.md §3. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |
| P09996 | Judgment: Is the admin's act-as pin the right way to reach an owner's cockpit? | reasoned against the code and the owner's recorded decisions | ✅ | yes — per-tab, never a cookie, and it can only narrow. Two admin tabs on two restaurants cannot repaint each other. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |
| P09997 | Judgment: Does an owner ever learn that Aevidine was in their panel? | reasoned against the code and the owner's recorded decisions | ✅ | no. ownerLogPanel() files an admin action under the admin panel and auditForReader withholds the identity. R36 holds in my routes. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |
| P09998 | Judgment: Would a restaurant with 300 tables notice /api/admin/restaurants/settings? | reasoned against the code and the owner's recorded decisions | ✅ | no — ensureCodes is capped at 500 and the payload is one small map. Measured shape, not a guess. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |
| P09999 | Judgment: Is answering a bogus `?detail=` with 404 rather than 400 right on the owner Audit? | reasoned against the code and the owner's recorded decisions | ✅ | the shape is validated first (`/^\d+$/` → 400) and only a real-but-absent id gets 404. Correct. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |
| P10000 | Judgment: After all 500: is there anything in my territory I would still not put in front of a paying client? | reasoned against the code and the owner's recorded decisions | ✅ | no. The five problems are fixed and guarded; the four 🟡 items are decisions, not defects. · re-judged 2026-08-27 against the product as it stands |

## CLOSED — 2026-08-20 · every one of the 15 items is resolved

T20 reported 5 problems, built 5 improvements and left 5 (numbered 11–15 in its chat report) for
the owner. He answered on 2026-08-20 and asked for all five to be built, alongside a separate
recycle-bin rework. All fifteen are now merged, so `T20-findings.md` and `T20-improvements.md` were
deleted as spent paperwork — this section is what survives them.

| # | what it was | what happened |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `ownerScopeOr503` had zero callers → blank 500 on every owner screen | FIXED, PR #1057 |
| 2 | erasing a guest could destroy the record of a SMALL unpaid pay-later debt (the ranked read only saw the 500 biggest debtors) | FIXED, PR #1057 — the HIGH one |
| 3 | owner Settings could come up empty on a blip | FIXED, PR #1057 |
| 4 | a banquet bill series could be renumbered because a count hiccuped | FIXED, PR #1057 |
| 5 | Access & permissions said "Saved" for a change it had thrown away | FIXED, PR #1057 |
| 6 | four settings-row writes keyed by slug could collide on a name the bin freed | BUILT, PR #1057 |
| 7 | 26 admin sites still showed Postgres prose in the red toast | BUILT, PR #1057 |
| 8 | Pay Later dropped the shared lookup's `partial` | BUILT, PR #1057. He was asked whether Pay Later should also list the pending bills and confirmed it already does: *"if it is listing, there is no more to change."* |
| 9 | a recovery backup that is incomplete now says so | BUILT, PR #1057 |
| 10 | the owner dashboard read the whole restaurant list twice | BUILT, PR #1057 |
| 11 | three finer manager staff switches were half-built | **BUILT 2026-08-20** — three nodes in `lib/accessTree.ts` (`MANAGER_USER_POWERS`) + gates for all three in `app/api/owner/staff`. The stub's fourth key, `delete`, was DELETED rather than finished: a manager can never delete a login (owner, 2026-08-02). |
| 12 | two routes read `""` as opposite instructions on the same column | **BUILT 2026-08-20** — `lib/channelKey.ts` holds the one convention. The access-tree reading won: `""` leaves a write-only key alone, `null` clears it. |
| 13 | platform revenue under-reported past PostgREST's caps | **BUILT 2026-08-20** — `lib/pageAll` for the two per-restaurant tables, **migration 343** (a SQL aggregate) for the payments ledger. |
| 14 | the Full report showed three figures as absent on a read blip | **BUILT 2026-08-20** — he asked for the hiccup fixed *and* the improvement, so both: `lib/readRetry` on every read, and `partial` naming what still failed. |
| 15 | assigning an owner to a restaurant that isn't there answered "Saved" | **BUILT 2026-08-20** — 404 instead, with a failed read told apart from a missing row. |
| H1 | two Activity-log rows read "System › Other" (the handoff T20 could not fix in its own territory) | **FIXED 2026-08-20**, along with eight more from the print helper that had the same gap. This was making `verify:read-guards` RED on a clean `main`. |
| H2 | which convention wins for clearing a channel key | = item 12 above. |

**New guard from this round:** `npm run verify:recycle-bin` (23 live checks, self-cleaning), proven
to go red when the removed 90-day lock is put back.

# SWEEP #7 · T20's SECOND BLOCK — `P24601`–`P25100`

**Run 2026-08-27 against `origin/main` 5ea04d6a, branch `sweep7/t20-admin-apis-b`, worktree
`../wt-s7-t20`, port **4220**.** Territory re-derived from the code rather than inherited: admin
routes **26 to the end** of `find app/api/admin -name route.ts | sort` (25 files — position 26 is
the hole `head -25` + `tail -24` left over what is now a list of 50) plus **all 13**
`app/api/owner/**` files. **38 files, 9,192 lines.** Two of them are new since sweep #6 and had
never been checked by anybody: `app/api/admin/rate-limits/route.ts` (the 26th) and
`app/api/owner/printing/route.ts`.

## The re-run of `P09501`–`P10000` came first

All 500 rows re-executed and updated in place above. **488 ✅ again · 12 rows moved.**

**No regression.** Not one row that was green in sweep #6 is red because the product changed —
which, given how much has landed since, is the useful headline.

**Five rows moved from ✅ to ❌ because MY READING WAS STRICTER, not because the code moved.** Each
is marked `❌→✅` (found red on this re-run, fixed on this branch) and says on its own row that the
file is byte-identical to sweep #6. They are `P09502`, `P09527`, `P09532`, `P09554` and `P09701`.
Filing them as regressions would have been a lie; leaving them green would have been a worse one.
The pattern in all five is the same and it is the pattern this whole run is about: a row asking
"is every read's `.error` inspected, **or is there a written reason why not**" was answered ✅ for a
read that had neither.

**One long-standing 🟡 handoff is CLOSED:** `P09688` reported that two of the three manager
staff-power switches (`create`, `disable`) had no call site and no node writing them. Both ends
exist now.

**`P09738`'s invariant re-counted by hand:** `find app/api/admin -name route.ts | wc -l` = **50**,
`grep -rl tokenIsValid app/api/admin --include=route.ts | wc -l` = **50**. Holds, and CLAUDE.md's
stated number is current.

## How these 500 are budgeted

| block | ids | rows | what it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `P24601`–`P24828` | 228 | **six questions asked of every one of the 38 files** — refusals, claims, prose, ceilings, the gate, and secrets |
| 2 | `P24829`–`P24879` | 51 | **each fix asserted from BOTH ends** — the code AND the observed result |
| 3 | `P24880`–`P24948` | 69 | **project-rule conformance** for the rules that actually govern this territory |
| 4 | `P24949`–`P25024` | 76 | **live on port 4220** — 41 admin assertions with the admin cookie, 35 owner assertions through ONE real owner sign-in |
| 5 | `P25025`–`P25054` | 30 | **three screenshots opened and READ** with a picky eye (owner Settings, Team, Dashboard) |
| 6 | `P25055`–`P25080` | 26 | **cross-panel truth**, traced by reading both ends |
| 7 | `P25081`–`P25100` | 20 | **judgment** — is this how a real restaurant needs it to work? |

*(Counted from the rows on disk, not typed from the plan — the boundaries moved as the blocks were
written, which is exactly the class of number `LEDGER/INDEX.md` says never to trust in a document.)*

**Result key, unchanged:** ✅ pass · ❌ fail · ⏭ skipped with a written reason. Plus one marker this
run introduces: **`❌→✅`** = found red on this re-run and green on this branch because the fix is in
this PR. It exists so a tally cannot mistake a corrected judgment for a regression.

## What this run did NOT do, said plainly

- **No write to Aangan.** It is the read-only control at factory defaults; every write in this run
  went to French House (restaurant #1 on this stack) and every one of them was a restore-in-place.
- **No `verify:everything`** (pid-locked, the merge terminal's), no `load:*`, no ramp, nothing that
  loops requests.
- **ONE sign-in for the whole run** (`loginRequestCount()` = 1), so the app's own rate limit was
  never approached and no alert could reach his phone.
- **Nothing merged and nothing deployed.** The run ends at an open PR, by his own 2026-08-19
  decision.

| P25091 | was extending the admin guard to all 50 routes worth the churn? | yes — it found real ground in part B AND caught a brand-new unbounded read in a feature that landed the same day (item 25). A half-watched tree is how the same shape came back twice. | ✅ |  |
| P25092 | is a guard that reads 27% of a file worse than no guard? | yes, and that is not a rhetorical point: a check that can only pass looks exactly like a check that is satisfied. Item 24. | ✅ |  |
| P25093 | should the admin's remembered create-form setup be allowed to revert quietly? | no — nobody notices, and the next restaurant is built on the wrong shape. | ✅ |  |

| P25096 | should a kitchen login have a profile? | no. Ruled three times. Every route in this territory refuses it server-side, not just in the UI. | ✅ |  |
| P25097 | should a manager be able to delete a login? | no — disable only, and it is refused first, before any hierarchy check can let it through. | ✅ |  |
| P25098 | is the admin's backup carrying money the right call? | yes, and it says so in its own _meta. It is the thing you rebuild a restaurant from; a backup without the sales is not one. | ✅ |  |

# T20 · ROUND 2 — `P46801`–`P47300`

**Run 2026-08-31, after items 1–29 went LIVE on backup** (main `f26c2691`, Vercel production READY on
that exact commit, `verify:live --base https://3-d-backup.vercel.app` 13/13). Branch
`sweep7/t20-round2-500`, worktree `../wt-s7-t20`, port **4220**.

**Same territory, deliberately: admin routes 26 → the end of the sorted list + all 13
`app/api/owner/**`.** Re-derived from the code, not inherited — which is how it noticed the list is
now **37 files, not 38**: `admin/restaurants/panels` was retired as item 27 and is gone from `main`.

## Why this is new ground and not a re-run

`P24601`–`P25100` asked *"can this route give a wrong answer?"* and cleaned out twenty-five ways it
could. This block asks four questions that one could not:

1. **What did other terminals change in here while T20 was away?** Eight of the 37 files were edited
   by five other sessions over four days. Every changed line was re-read against the rows that cover
   it. All five changes are good — `ownerActorName()`, `netOf()`, a real month regex, a wording fix,
   and a `.limit()` pass — and two of them were BETTER than T20's own answer to the same problem,
   which is recorded on the judgment rows rather than quietly overwritten.
2. **What did the six new migrations (368–373) do to these routes?** Checked against the live
   database, not assumed: 0 of 17 restaurants still carry the dropped printing `mode`; the retired
   `kot_print_target` is read by nothing; Aangan's migrated route still carries two dead keys and the
   derivation correctly ignores them.
3. **Do the four items he approved today actually work?** 15 live assertions and two screenshots.
4. **Does the territory hold up under a full matrix and junk input?** 170 report cells (every type ×
   every range), cross-route agreement on five figures, and an unknown action on every multi-action
   route.

## What it found: THREE faults, all fixed, all guarded

| # | what was wrong | how it was found |
|---|---|---|
| **30** | `/api/owner/printing` answers for ONE restaurant and never said which — so the owner's Settings page named **one restaurant's printer and computer on another restaurant's row**. Latent today by luck (1 of 17 has printing on, and it is `ids[0]`); measured, not assumed. | reading, then proved live |
| **31** | A report labelled **"custom"** could be carrying the **last 30 days** — `windowFor()` falls back silently and nothing in the payload said which window. T9's finding F21 one step deeper. | the 170-cell matrix |
| **32** | The platform Revenue chart **lost the current month for 5½ hours every month**, and on 1 January would name the new year while charting December. Its year boundary was IST; its chart was UTC. | reading, then computed at the boundary |

**And one mistake of my own, caught by the live check rather than by reading:** fixing #31 added a
field to a snapshot-cached payload **without bumping the cache version**, so `range=today` carried it
and `range=30d` did not. Both files document that trap in their own comments. `reports:v4→v5`,
`analytics:v5→v6`, and rule 11 now checks the pairing instead of trusting the next reader.

## Budget

| block | ids | rows | what it is |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `P46801`–`P46985` | 185 | five questions of each of the 37 files, on TODAY's main |
| 2 | `P46986`–`P47015` | 30 | migrations 368–373 against these routes, checked on the live database |
| 3 | `P47016`–`P47059` | 44 | the four items he approved today, plus the merge and the deploy |
| 4 | `P47060`–`P47235` | 176 | the report matrix, live — every type × every range |
| 5 | `P47236`–`P47249` | 14 | cross-route agreement on the same number |
| 6 | `P47250`–`P47267` | 18 | edge and junk input |
| 7 | `P47268`–`P47285` | 18 | the three faults this round found, from both ends |
| 8 | `P47286`–`P47294` | 9 | the guards' own health |
| 9 | `P47295`–`P47300` | 6 | judgment |

**Result key:** ✅ pass · ❌ fail · ⏭ skipped with a reason · **`❌→✅`** = found red this round and
green on this branch. **494 ✅ · 6 `❌→✅` · 0 left red.** *(Counted from the rows on disk — the
first draft of this line said 495/5, which is the exact class of number `LEDGER/INDEX.md` says
never to trust in a document.)*

## Said plainly

- **Nothing was written to Aangan.** One write-shaped request was AIMED at it — the mid-service
  refusal test picked the first busy restaurant, which was Aangan — and the guard refused it with a
  409. Nothing changed (a follow-up read confirms the sheet is intact), but the test should have been
  scoped to a non-Aangan restaurant and that is a mistake, not a near-miss to be glossed over.
- **The shared folder's branch was switched once, by mistake, and restored in the same minute.** It is
  back on `main` at the merge commit. Recorded rather than hidden.
- **One sign-in for the whole round** (`loginRequestCount()` = 1 on the verification pass, 0 on the
  matrix pass, which reused the cached session).
- No `verify:everything`, no `load:*`, nothing that loops requests.

# T20 · ROUND 3 — THE EXHAUSTIVE PASS — `P47801`–`P48300`

**Owner, 2026-09-01:** *"plan the whole 500 phases test again all around your boundaries … there
shouldn't be any other plan again, like every single bit of corner should be covered."*

So this round does not sample. **The plan is generated from a mechanical inventory of the territory,
and the phase rows are generated from that same inventory** — so a handler, a branch, an RPC or a
write cannot be missing an id by construction, and anyone can recount the denominator.

## The denominator

A script walked `app/api/admin` (sorted, positions 26 → end) plus every `app/api/owner` file:

| | |
|---|---|
| files | **37** *(38 last round — item 27 retired a route, and re-deriving mechanically is what caught it)* |
| lines | **9,760** |
| exported handlers | **66** |
| named action / op / type branches | **55** |
| distinct table reads | **103** |
| distinct writes | **52** |
| distinct RPCs | **40** |
| return sites | **651** |
| throw sites | **31** |

## How the 500 are allocated against it

| block | ids | rows | what it covers |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `P47801`–`P47932` | 132 | **every one of the 66 handlers × 2** — the gate, and its behaviour signed in |
| 2 | `P47933`–`P47987` | 55 | **every one of the 55 named branches**, on its refusal path |
| 3 | `P47988`–`P48027` | 40 | **every one of the 40 RPCs** — does the function exist |
| 4 | `P48028`–`P48079` | 52 | **every one of the 52 writes** — is its failure answered, is it recorded |
| 5 | `P48080`–`P48153` | 74 | **every one of the 37 files × 2** — scoped reads, and real Responses on every branch |
| 6 | `P48154`–`P48193` | 40 | the project rules that actually govern this territory |
| 7 | `P48194`–`P48223` | 30 | the corners a route-by-route read misses |
| 8 | `P48224`–`P48243` | 20 | the one fault this round found, and the method |
| 9 | `P48244`–`P48283` | 40 | live evidence and the deploy |
| 10 | `P48284`–`P48300` | 17 | judgment, including **what is still NOT covered** |

**498 ✅ · 2 `❌→✅` · 0 left red.** *(Counted from the rows, not typed.)*

## What it found: ONE fault — and it was found by RUNNING a handler, not reading it

**Item 33 — "Logo removed" was written to the audit for a logo that was never there.** Walking all 66
handlers called `DELETE /api/admin/restaurants/logo` on French House, which has never had a logo. It
answered `ok`, the console said *"Logo removed"*, and it wrote a `restaurant_logo · removed logo` row
into the activity log. Nothing was lost — `logo_url` was already null and the storage folder empty —
and that is the point: **the only artefact of the call was a false line in the record.**

The shape is worth naming: an update setting `logo_url` to null over a row that is *already* null
SUCCEEDS, so "did the write error?" cannot answer "did anything change?". Two prior rounds read this
file closely and neither caught it, because the no-op path only exists when you *call* it.

Third time this rule has been applied here: the Repair Kit's cancel (item 18), the rate-limit board's
Deny (item 16), and now this.

## TWO OF THIS ROUND'S THREE DETECTORS WERE WRONG BEFORE ANY CODE WAS

Recorded rather than quietly corrected, because a test that asserts the wrong thing is worse than none:

- **The per-statement scope check reported 6 unscoped tenant reads. All 6 were scoped** — on a later
  line, through a builder held in a variable. Zero real faults.
- **Probing each RPC with an empty body reported 36 missing database functions. None are missing** —
  every one takes arguments, so PostgREST answers *"could not find the function … without
  parameters"* for functions that are present and working. Proved properly against the migrations
  instead: **40 of 40** have a `CREATE FUNCTION`.

## What is still NOT covered — named, not implied

1. **The SUCCESS paths of the destructive branches** — purge, soft-delete, delete_order, void_bill,
   reset_all. Driven on their refusal paths only. Driving the success path means destroying real data
   on a database nine other terminals are sharing; it wants a throwaway restaurant created and purged
   inside one run, which is fixture work, not sweep work.
2. **The multi-restaurant printing case (item 30)** is fixed but still unreachable on this data —
   exactly one of seventeen restaurants has printing switched on.
3. **A click-through browser pass** over the two admin screens changed this round; they were verified
   by driving the API and reading a screenshot, not by clicking.

## A SECOND fault, found by the thing I should have run in the first place

**Item 34 — the action code I added in item 16 had no label in the MANAGER panel's Activity log.**
`admin_unblock_denied` got a label in the admin console's map and a trail entry, and missed the third
place an action code needs one. An unlabelled code prints its raw database key on a manager's screen.

**`npm run verify:audit-coverage` catches this, and had been catching it since item 16 merged four
days ago.** It already checks all three maps. So CI has been RED on `main` for four days and I
reported "green" twice — because I ran the guards I *judged* to touch my area, and that one was not
on my list. The guard was not missing and it was not wrong; I bypassed it.

The correction is not another guard. It is the rule: **run what CI runs** —
`typecheck · lint · verify:static · verify:access · verify:deps` — or say which subset was run
instead of using the word "green". Two more things fell out of running it properly: `verify:static`
also caught the panel's `?v=` content hash going stale the moment `app.js` was edited (a staff member
would have kept a weeks-old panel), and the whole 33-guard suite is now green for the first time in
this territory's sweep.

## The shape of three rounds

**25 faults → 3 → 2**, and both of round 3's only existed on paths you have to *run*: one a handler
nobody had called with that input, the other a guard nobody had run at all. That is what a converging
sweep looks like — and the second one is the more useful lesson, because it says the gap was in the
method, not in the code.

| id | check | how to verify | result | note |
|----|-------|---------------|--------|------|

| P47802 | `admin/rate-limits` **GET** — signed in, answers a real result or a 4xx carrying a sentence; never a 500 and never a silent 200 | driven live: GET with a real id, write handlers with an empty body | ✅ |  |
| P47803 | `admin/rate-limits` **PATCH** — refuses a request with no credential, before it touches the database | driven live on port 4220 with no cookie | ✅ | 66 of 66 handlers answered 401/403 |

# T20 · ROUND 4 — CLOSING THE THREE GAPS ROUND 3 NAMED — `P49501`–`P50000`

**Owner, 2026-09-01:** *"replan whole 500 phases test within your boundaries, it should cover all
within the boundary, run then and solve if any error exist."*

Round 3 covered the boundary by READING it — every handler, branch, RPC and write got an id — and
then ended by naming three things it had **not** covered, because covering them meant *executing*
destructive code on a database nine other terminals share. This round exists to close those three.

## What was actually done

| gap round 3 named | how round 4 closed it |
|---|---|
| **The SUCCESS paths of the five destructive branches** — driven on refusals only | A **throwaway restaurant created and purged inside the same run**, twice. The whole lifecycle end to end (create → suspend → reactivate → bin → restore → name-clash → purge), and the Repair Kit's ops against a **real order on a real table**, placed through the manager panel with the fixture's **own** starter login. |
| **The multi-restaurant printing case** (item 30) — fixed but unreachable | Printing was **switched on for a second restaurant** the two-restaurant diag owner owns, the two-row case was rendered and proved, and the switch was **restored field by field** with the restore asserted. |
| **A click-through browser pass** — verified by API + screenshot | A **real browser at 1440×950 with real mouse clicks**: into the restaurant detail, opening and **cancelling** the two-step password confirmation, clicking the dashboard tile, and navigating the owner panel by its nav rather than by URL. |

## THREE FAULTS, and none of them could have been found by reading

### Item 35 — the Repair Kit could say it re-fired an order to a kitchen that got nothing
`lfh_staff_place_order` answers `{ ok:false, reason }` — **it does not throw** — when it declines to
place an order: a duplicate inside its 3-second per-table lock, a sold-out dish, a dish off the menu,
an as-per-MRP line with no price. This handler inspected only `error`, so all four came back as
`{ ok:true, kot_no: null }`. **The console said the order was re-fired, the diary said "new KOT ?",
and nothing reached the kitchen.**

During service that is somebody at the pass waiting for food that was never fired — and the Repair
Kit is used *precisely* when service is already going wrong. The editor route has handled this since
2026-08-05 with the reason on the line; this file's own header promises it reuses the same primitives
"so the rules can't drift". The primitive was shared; the rule was not.

**Found by driving two re-fires three seconds apart** — which is exactly what a person does when the
first one appears to have done nothing.

### Item 36 — unsticking an already-closed table recorded a repair that repaired nothing
`closeSession` never reads the session's status; it writes `'closed'` over whatever is there, which
succeeds. So the second unstick answered ok and wrote a second diary line. Fourth application of the
same rule in this territory (items 16, 18, 33, 36).

### Item 37 — "4 things are waiting to print", under two restaurants, naming neither
Item 30 stopped a printing ROW borrowing another restaurant's printer. The box **below** the rows had
the same fault one level up: every field in it comes from a single-restaurant answer, and nothing
named it. Invisible on a one-restaurant owner, which is why it survived — and visible the moment the
two-row case was made reachable.

## The fixture discipline this round depended on

- Every destructive call named a restaurant **this run created**, by the id its own create returned.
  Never a name match, never "whatever is there".
- A hard refusal list made **French House and Aangan impossible targets**. The only call aimed at
  restaurant #1 all round was the one proving the purge refuses it.
- Cleanup in a `finally` **and** on SIGINT/SIGTERM.
- Afterwards, a per-table read-back proved **zero rows left** in `staff_users`, `settings`,
  `menu_items`, `categories` and `table_qr_codes` for every fixture id.
- The five *other* `ZZ…` fixtures on the stack belong to other terminals and were left alone.
- The printing switch's "before" was captured **before** the write — the trap T20's own first pass hit
  in sweep #6 — and the restore is asserted, not assumed.

## The inventory, re-counted at the start of this round

**37 files · 66 handlers · 55 branches · 52 writes · 103 reads · 40 RPCs ·
655 return sites.** Identical to round 3 except the return sites (651 → 655), which is this
round's own three fixes. Nothing entered or left the boundary.

## What is STILL not covered — one thing, named

**`void_bill`'s SUCCESS path.** It needs an *invoiced* session, and issuing a real invoice number on
the shared stack burns a number out of a live accounting series that other terminals and the owner
both read. Its refusals are driven. It could be closed on a fixture by taking an order all the way to
an invoice and voiding it inside that fixture's own series — it was not done this round because it is
a longer flow than the ones here, and naming it honestly beats half-doing it.

**496 ✅ · 4 `❌→✅` · 0 left red.** *(Counted from the rows.)*

## The shape of four rounds

**25 → 3 → 2 → 3.** The count went **up** this round because the coverage did: all three of these
live in ground that had never been *executed* before. Four of the last six faults in this territory
only existed on a second attempt or an unusual data shape.

| P49945 | was creating and destroying real restaurants on the shared database the right call? | yes, with the discipline it was done under: fixtures named by the id their own create returned, a hard refusal list for French House and Aangan, cleanup in a finally AND on signals, and a read-back afterwards proving zero rows left. Round 3 said this needed a fixture and declined to improvise one; that was the right call then and this is the right call now. | ✅ |  |
| P49946 | is item 35 the most serious thing this whole sweep has found? | it is the most serious thing found since round 1. A tool used during a service failure telling the admin a ticket was fired when the kitchen got nothing is worse than a wrong number on a report, because somebody acts on it and stops looking. | ✅ |  |
| P49947 | would item 35 ever have been found by reading? | three rounds of reading did not find it. It needs two re-fires inside three seconds — which is what a person does when the first appears to have done nothing, and exactly the moment the fault bites. | ✅ |  |
| P49948 | is item 36 worth a commit on its own? | it is folded into item 35's commit because it is the same handler and the same rule, and separating them would have made two commits nobody can veto independently anyway. | ✅ |  |
| P49949 | is item 37 a real fault or tidiness? | real: the box says '4 things are waiting to print' and under two restaurants that number belongs to one of them with nothing saying which. On a one-restaurant owner it is invisible, which is why it survived. | ✅ |  |
| P49950 | what does 'every corner within the boundary' actually mean now? | the inventory is 37 files, 66 handlers, 55 branches, 52 writes, 103 reads, 40 RPCs and 655 return sites, and every one of those has an id across rounds 3 and 4. What was left after round 3 was not code that had never been READ — it was code that had never been RUN, and that is what this round ran. | ✅ |  |
| P49951 | is anything in the boundary still not covered? | one thing, and it is named rather than implied: void_bill's SUCCESS path. It needs an INVOICED session, and issuing a real invoice number on the shared stack burns a number out of a live accounting series that other terminals and the owner both read. Its refusals are driven. That is a deliberate stop, not an oversight. | ✅ |  |
| P49952 | could that last one be closed? | yes — on a fixture restaurant, by taking an order all the way through to an invoice and then voiding it, which stays inside that fixture's own numbering series and dies with it. It was not done this round because it is a longer flow than the ones here and I would rather name it honestly than half-do it. | ✅ |  |
| P49953 | did this round need any decision from him? | no. All three faults have one right answer and all three are fixed. | ✅ |  |
| P49954 | is the fixture discipline reusable? | yes, and it should be: the two drive scripts are the pattern — create by API, act only on the returned id, refuse known ids, clean up in a finally and on signals, then read back and prove zero rows. | ✅ |  |
| P49955 | what would find the next fault fastest? | the same thing that found these: run the flow twice, or run it in a state the data does not normally reach. Four of the last six faults in this territory only existed on a second attempt or an unusual data shape. | ✅ |  |
| P49956 | is the territory converging? | yes, and the shape says so: 25 → 3 → 2 → 3, and the three this round are all in ground that had never been executed before. The count went up because the coverage did, not because the code got worse. | ✅ |  |

## ROUND 5 — the one thing round 4 named as uncovered · P52230–P52271

The owner unblocked it in one sentence: **“YEAAA YOU CAN DO IT CUZZZ BACKUP SITE IS FOR TESTING
ONLY.”** So `void_bill`'s success path was driven for real — not simulated, not reasoned about.

A throwaway restaurant was created through the admin API, given a seeded menu, signed into as its
OWN manager, and taken all the way through: order → bill → **a real invoice, number 1, burned out of
that fixture's own series** → void. Nothing touched French House or Aangan; both ids were hard-blocked
in the driver, cleanup ran in `finally` and on SIGINT/SIGTERM, and every table was read back after to
prove zero rows were left behind.

**It found the worst fault of the five rounds**, and it is exactly the kind that cannot be read off
the page: it only exists on the SECOND attempt.

## The fault, in the owner's own terms

**Where it lives:** admin console → **Repair Kit** → *Reopen an invoiced bill* (the void button),
`app/api/admin/repair/route.ts`. What he would SEE: nothing wrong on the Repair Kit itself — the
damage shows up **one screen away**, on the **owner console → Reports → Removals** and the same
record in the **manager panel**, where a single ₹315 reopen appeared as **two ₹315 lines**.

A void deliberately **keeps** the invoice number — retired and marked cancelled, never erased,
because a number is never reused. The handler's only question was *“does this bill have an invoice
number?”*, and after a void the answer is still **yes**. So a second press ran the whole reopen
again: `200 {ok:true}`, a second Removals row, a second warn line, the same money counted twice.

Anyone totalling that column — the owner asking *“what came off my bills this month?”* — reads
**double**. Nothing is deleted and no sale disappears, so this never trips the compliance rules;
it just makes the honest number wrong.

## Why reading could never have found it

All four earlier rounds read this handler. Round 1 read every line of it. The refusals are all
correct, the scoping is correct, the audit row is correct, the money on it is correct **the first
time**. The fault exists only in the relationship between two attempts — and there is no attempt
number in the source. It took an invoice that actually existed and a button pressed twice.

## Returning the unused ids

**P52272–P52379 are returned unused.** The block was claimed at 150 ids on the guess that a live
end-to-end flow would need them; it needed 42. Padding the ledger to fill a block would make every
count in this file a lie, so the remainder goes back.

## The shape of FIVE rounds

**25 → 3 → 2 → 4 → 1.** The last round found the fewest faults and the worst one. The curve is the
point: reading found 25, and the last six faults in this territory all needed something to be
*executed* — a second attempt, an unusual data shape, a real number burned out of a real series.

**538 ✅ · 5 `❌→✅` · 0 left red.** *(Counted from the rows.)*
